# Critical comparison of the Xylander and Vinet translations

2026-11-20 · OpenAI · GPT-6 (exact deployment identifier unavailable)

Three subagents drafted X01–X29, X30–X58 and V01–V33 respectively. The lead reviewer drafted V34–V66. The four groups were assigned to different reviewers for the adversarial pass, and the lead reviewer assembled and validated the comparison.

## Xylander

### X01 · Xyl. 40.1–2

*Exacta summa hic, evidensque Musicae.*

The heading announces an exact and clear compendium of music.

- **Russian base** · B. The Russian heading is compact and idiomatic.
- **English base** · A = C = D. All retain the announcement, accuracy, clarity, and summary.

### X02 · Xyl. 40.3–7

*Universum hoc, antiqui Musica contineri perhibuerunt. Nihil enim in rerum natura absque commensuratione et proportione consistit:*

The ancients maintained that music holds this universe together. Nothing in nature exists without due measure and proportion. The argument continues in X03.

- **Russian base** · D. The active construction conveys what holds the universe together.
- **English base** · C = D. Both preserve the cosmic claim and the two distinct measure terms.

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **C · doubt · open** (modified)
  - **Printed** · VNiuersum hoc, antiqui Musica / contineri perhibuerunt. Nihil e- / nim in rerum natura absque com- / mensuratione et proportione con- / sistit: neque quicquam eorum quæ
  - **Proposal** · Эта вселенная, как утверждали древние, заключена в музыке. / This universe, the ancients declared, is contained in music.
  - A bare ablative with contineor can be locative, as Allen and Greenough §431 specifically notes, so the grammar alone does not rule out ‘contained within music’. Here the following claim that due measure and proportion operate throughout nature and X07's account of music arranging all things support the stronger causal ‘held together by music’.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 40.3–7 [checked] · Allen and Greenough, New Latin Grammar, §§409 and 431 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. contineo A [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.2, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 187–189 [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **C** · Explain the sense of commensuratio and its coordination with proportio and proportionalitas.
- **D** · Boethius, De institutione musica I.2, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 187–189 [checked] gives a precise parallel for cosmic music in the heavens, elements, and seasons.

### X03 · Xyl. 40.7–12

*neque quicquam eorum quae fiunt, sine debita commensuratione et proportione si fiat, recte productum natumve esse dicitur: sive id ab arte efficiatur, sive a natura: et sive sub sensum illud cadat, sive solo intellectu perspiciatur.*

Nothing that comes about without due measure and proportion is said to have been properly produced or born, whether art or nature brings it about and whether sense or intellect alone apprehends it.

- **Russian base** · B. The conditional and both pairs are clear.
- **English base** · B = D. Both keep the condition and the two independent pairs of alternatives.

**Errors**

- **A · en · minor language** · “anything among the things that come to be said to have been”. The uninterrupted words invite a reading in which come to be governs said. The Latin separates quae fiunt from the main dicitur. **Fix** · Set the condition off before the main predicate, as in C. *(confirmed)*

### X04 · Xyl. 40.13–15

*Caeterum Musica ipsa per se commensuratio quaedam omnino et proportionalitas est, nimirum harmonia universi existens.*

Music itself, considered in itself, is assuredly a kind of due measure and proportionality, being the harmony of the universe.

- **Russian base** · B. Gives per se and allows omnino to reinforce the assertion.
- **English base** · D. Keeps in itself, both measure terms, and the explanatory participle.

**Divergence · commensuratio quaedam omnino et proportionalitas est**

Does omnino mark extent or reinforce the assertion?

- **A, C** · Extent, as all-round or complete measure.
  - A · RU «Музыка же сама по себе есть всецело некая соразмерность и пропорциональность, будучи, разумеется, гармонией вселенной.» / EN “Music itself, moreover, is in its own right altogether a certain commensuration and proportionality, being, to be sure, the harmony of the universe.”
  - C · RU «Музыка же сама по себе есть всецело некая соразмерность и пропорциональность: она, разумеется, и есть гармония вселенной.» / EN “Now music itself is in itself altogether a kind of commensuration and proportionality, being, indeed, the harmony of the universe.”
- **B, D** · Emphatic assertion that music is a kind of due measure and proportionality.
  - B · RU «Сама же музыка по себе, несомненно, есть своего рода соразмерность и пропорциональность, ибо она представляет собой гармонию вселенной.» / EN “Music in itself is assuredly a kind of commensurateness and proportionality, for it is the harmony of the universe.”
  - D · RU «Сама же музыка есть, без сомнения, некая соразмерность и пропорциональность, ибо она и есть гармония вселенной.» / EN “Music in itself is certainly a kind of commensuration and proportionality, being, of course, the harmony of the universe.”

**Verdict** · Both adverbial effects are possible. Emphasis on the assertion is clearer with quaedam, an indefinite qualification that remains operative.

- **Russian** · Музыка сама по себе, несомненно, есть своего рода соразмерность и пропорциональность.
- **English** · Music in itself is indeed a kind of due measure and proportionality.
- **Evidence** · Lewis & Short, s.v. omnino I and II.B [checked] · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 40.13–15 [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · medium · modified · for the editor

**Errors**

- **D · ru · minor omission** · “Сама же музыка есть, без сомнения”. ‘Сама’ conveys ipsa, but the separate per se qualification, explicitly retained in D's English, has no Russian equivalent. **Fix** · Use «Сама же музыка, взятая сама по себе, несомненно, есть…» or the less repetitive «Музыка же сама по себе несомненно есть…». *(added)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **B · doubt · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · Cæterùm Musica ipsa per se commensura- / tio quædam omnino et proportionalitas est, ni- / mirum harmonia uniuersi existens. Fortassis et
  - **Proposal** · Сама музыка по себе во всех отношениях есть своего рода соразмерность и пропорциональность. / Music itself is in every respect a kind of commensurateness and proportionality.
  - The general intensive sense supports the alternative's extent. The indefinite quaedam and the definition together favor the assertion-level reading, which is also a way to render the intensive without making a claim about separate respects.
  - **Evidence** · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. omnino I [checked] · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 40.13–15 [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Preserve the change from proportio to proportionalitas. The Boethian technical distinction concerns a relation of terms and the correspondence of ratios. Boethius, De institutione musica II.12–14, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 242–244 [checked]

**Editor questions**

- Choose the force of omnino in the main assertion. Keep the distinct forms proportio and proportionalitas visible across X02–X04.

### X05 · Xyl. 40.15–20

*Fortassis et ipsam divinam naturam haud inconvenienter sui ipsius et universi harmoniam quispiam dixerit: siquidem in Deo concinnatus, et ad consonantiam coaptatus mundus, bene, atque adeo quam optime se habere videtur.*

One might aptly even call divine nature itself the harmony of itself and the universe, since the world, ordered in God and fitted for consonance, seems to be in the best possible state.

- **Russian base** · A. Preserves the qualification and the emphatic superlative.
- **English base** · B = C. Both make the tentative comparison and the progression from well to as well as possible explicit.

**Errors**

- **B · ru · minor mistranslation** · “явно пребывает”. The formulation substitutes explicit certainty for the appearance expressed by videtur. **Fix** · по-видимому, пребывает *(confirmed)*

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Retain the distinction between concinnare and coaptare and trace the musical force of consonantia back into the cosmological claim.

### X06 · Xyl. 40.20–21

*Tanto nimirum Musicam illi dignati sunt miraculo.*

The greatness of the wonder the ancients accorded music follows from what has just been said.

- **Russian base** · D. Makes the anaphoric subject clear and conveys the ancients' judgment.
- **English base** · B = D. Convey dignari without a misleading suggestion of supernatural events.

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Link the sense of miraculum to Pliny's tree worthy of wonder, and identify illi as the ancients of X02. Lewis and Short, s.v. miraculum I and dignor [checked].

### X07 · Xyl. 40.21–41.3

*Ea quando in omnibus conspicitur, atque universa temperat, exornatque, tum sensui voluptatem, congruentiam autem (harmoniam dico) Naturae, beatitatem denique intellectui largitur: nos de ea Musica quae in sensibus, aut potius uno sensuum auditu consideratur, paucis disseremus.*

Music is discernible in all things, regulates and adorns them all, and gives pleasure to sense, accord (that is, harmony) to nature, and blessedness to intellect. The author will briefly discuss music considered in the senses, strictly in hearing.

- **Russian base** · B. Makes the audible domain explicit while keeping the methodological verb.
- **English base** · B. Preserves the progression from the general scope of music to the faculty that considers it.

**Divergence · quae in sensibus, aut potius uno sensuum auditu consideratur**

Is the final relative clause about music as an object of consideration or direct perception?

- **A, B, C** · Music considered by or in the domain of the senses, more precisely by hearing.
  - A · RU «Поскольку она усматривается во всём, всё упорядочивает и украшает и к тому же дарует чувству наслаждение, природе — согласованность (я разумею гармонию), а уму, наконец, — блаженство, мы кратко скажем о той музыке, которая рассматривается в чувствах, или, вернее, в одном из чувств — слухе.» / EN “Since it is seen in all things, and tempers and adorns the whole, and moreover bestows pleasure on sense, congruence (I mean harmony) on Nature, and lastly blessedness on the intellect, we shall discourse briefly of that Music which is considered in the senses, or rather in one of the senses, hearing.”
  - B · RU «Музыка обнаруживается во всём, всё соразмеряет и украшает, доставляет чувству удовольствие, природе — согласованность (я имею в виду гармонию), а разуму — блаженство. Поэтому мы вкратце поговорим о музыке, которую рассматривают посредством чувств, вернее, одного из них — слуха.» / EN “Since music is discernible in all things, tempers and adorns them all, and grants pleasure to sense, accord (I mean harmony) to nature, and finally blessedness to the intellect, we shall speak briefly of music as considered by the senses, or rather by one of them, hearing.”
  - C · RU «Поскольку она усматривается во всём, всё умеряет и украшает и дарует чувству наслаждение, природе — согласованность (я разумею гармонию), а уму, наконец, — блаженство, мы вкратце поведём речь о той музыке, которая рассматривается в чувствах, или, вернее, в одном из чувств — в слухе.» / EN “Since it is seen in all things, and tempers and adorns all things, and bestows pleasure on sense, congruence (I mean harmony) on Nature, and finally blessedness on the intellect, we shall discuss briefly that music which is considered in the senses, or rather in the one sense of hearing.”
- **D** · Music directly perceived by the senses, more precisely by hearing.
  - D · RU «Поскольку музыка обнаруживается во всём, всему даёт строй и всё украшает, а также дарует чувству удовольствие, природе — согласие (я разумею гармонию), а уму, наконец, — блаженство, мы вкратце скажем о той музыке, которую воспринимают чувствами, вернее, одним из чувств — слухом.» / EN “Since music is discernible in all things, brings all things into due order and adorns them, and gives pleasure to sense, agreement (I mean harmony) to nature and, finally, blessedness to the intellect, we shall speak briefly of the music that is perceived by the senses, or, more precisely, by one of them, hearing.”

**Verdict** · Both concern the auditory domain. Consideratur favors examination or consideration, in keeping with disseremus, and retains a verbal distinction from conspicitur at the beginning.

- **Russian** · о той музыке, которую рассматривают посредством чувств — точнее, посредством одного из них, слуха
- **English** · of music as considered through the senses, or more precisely through one of them, hearing
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 40.21–41.3 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. considero I–II [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.2, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 187–189 [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · medium · confirmed

**Errors**

- **A · notes · major note-claim** · “A threefold division: music of sense, of nature and of the intellect”. The single subject music confers three gifts on three recipients. The clause does not divide music into those three classes as Boethius expressly does with his different criteria in I.2. **Fix** · Distinguish the three effects and the narrower auditory subject of the ensuing exposition. *(confirmed)*
- **D · both · minor mistranslation** · “music that is perceived by the senses / музыку, которую воспринимают чувствами”. Consideratur introduces the domain in which the author will examine music. Direct perception shifts the focus away from considering music under its auditory aspect. **Fix** · Use ‘music as considered in the domain of sense, more precisely of hearing’ / «музыка, рассматриваемая в области чувственного восприятия, точнее слуха». *(added)*

**Notes to keep**

- **C** · Keep the lexical note on beatitas and Vinet's beatitudo, subject to verification of any claim of first coinage.
- **D** · Music brings things into proper measure and Xylander glosses congruentia with harmonia.

### X08 · Xyl. 41.4–5

*Sonus est, vocis nullo intervallo divisae harmoniae particeps intensio, seu tenor.*

A musical note is the pitch-tension, or sustained tone, of voice undivided by any interval and capable of entering harmony.

- **Russian base** · B. Makes the pitch and the attachment of particeps explicit.
- **English base** · B = D. Distinguish pitch from its sustained-tone gloss and avoid ambiguity about what partakes of harmony.

**Divergence · intensio, seu tenor**

How does the doublet define a note?

- **A** · Tension and actual holding of the voice.
  - A · RU «Звук есть причастное гармонии напряжение, или удержание, голоса, не разделённого никаким интервалом.» / EN “A note is a tension, or sustained holding, of a voice divided by no interval, [a tension] that partakes of harmony.”
- **C** · Tension and pitch of the voice.
  - C · RU «Звук есть причастное гармонии напряжение, или высота, голоса, не разделённого никаким интервалом.» / EN “A note is the tension, or pitch, of a voice undivided by any interval, [a tension] partaking of harmony.”
- **B, D** · Pitch and its steady or sustained sounding.
  - B · RU «Звук — это высота, или выдерживаемый тон, голосового звучания, не разделённого никаким интервалом, причём эта высота причастна гармонии.» / EN “A note is a pitch, or sustained tone, of vocal sound undivided by any interval, a pitch that partakes of harmony.”
  - D · RU «Звук — это высота голоса, не разделённого никаким интервалом, иначе говоря, его ровное звучание, причастное гармонии.» / EN “A note is the pitch, or steady tone, of a voice undivided by any interval, a pitch that partakes of harmony.”

**Verdict** · Boethius places the voice on one intensio, supporting pitch as the value of the term here. Tenor can mean holding or tone. Sustained tone captures both strands without implying strain or emphasizing duration above constancy of pitch.

- **Russian** · высота, или выдерживаемый тон, голоса
- **English** · the pitch, or sustained tone, of the voice
- **Evidence** · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. tenor I (Quintilian 1.5.22, 26) [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked] · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.4–5 [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · confirmed

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **A · doubt · unsound** (confirmed)
  - **Printed** · Sonus est, uocis nullo interuallo diuisæ har- / moniæ particeps intensio, seu tenor. Interuallum
  - **Proposal** · Звук есть напряжение, или удержание, голоса, причастное гармонии, не разделённой никаким интервалом. / A note is a tension, or sustained holding, of the voice that partakes of a harmony divided by no interval.
  - Genitive divisae can formally agree with either noun. X12 immediately expounds indivisibility as a property of the note and separately expounds participation in harmony. The grammar and that sequence favor divisae with vocis.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.4–5 and 41.9–13 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked]
- **B · doubt · sound** (confirmed)
  - **Printed** · Sonus est, uocis nullo interuallo diuisæ har- / moniæ particeps intensio, seu tenor. Interuallum
  - **Proposal** · Звук — причастная гармонии высота, или устойчивый уровень высоты, голосового звучания, не разделённого никаким интервалом. / A note is a pitch, or steady level of pitch, of vocal sound undivided by any interval, which participates in harmony.
  - Steady level of pitch is supported by tenor in Quintilian and by the reference to a single pitch. The supplied alternative's final English relative clause needs recasting so that particeps qualifies the pitch, the head of the Latin definition.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.4–5 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. tenor I (Quintilian 1.5.22, 26) [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **B** · Keep Boethius's definition by one pitch suitable for melody. Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked]
- **A** · Discuss the doublet and explain that the indivisibility and the capacity to enter harmony are distinct points in X12.

### X09 · Xyl. 41.5–7

*Intervallum autem, sonorum mutua unius ad alterum ratio, sive respectus.*

An interval is the mutual ratio or relation of one note to another.

- **Russian base** · D. Renders the relative ordering and the doublet naturally.
- **English base** · D. States both what is related and that the relation is mutual.

**Notes to keep**

- **C** · Explain the technical relational meaning of respectus and Vinet's different term habitudo.
- **D** · Boethius defines the interval by distance, in contrast with Xylander's relational definition. Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked]

### X10 · Xyl. 41.7–8

*Systema, complexus est certus intervallorum.*

A system is a definite combination of intervals.

- **Russian base** · B = C = D. All give the same clear translation.
- **English base** · B = C = D. All distinguish a definite configuration of intervals.

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **C · doubt · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · siue respectus. Systema, complexus est certus / interuallorum. Harmonia, ipsa constructio sy-
  - **Proposal** · Система — некое сочетание интервалов. / A system is a certain combination of intervals.
  - A certain is lexically defensible if read as particular or definite. An indefinite 'some combination' would obscure the force of certus. The versions need not be made to read identically.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.7–8 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. certus I [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · The later tetrachord, pentachord, and octachord illustrate systems with different extents.

### X11 · Xyl. 41.8–9

*Harmonia, ipsa constructio systematum.*

Harmony is the actual arrangement of systems.

- **Russian base** · A = B = C = D. The four Russian versions coincide.
- **English base** · A = B = C = D. Actual and very convey ipsa equivalently.

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Connect the definition with X47, where constructing and fitting together systems is the proper work of harmony.

### X12 · Xyl. 41.9–13

*Enimvero sonus divisionis capax non est, ut neque punctum in Geometria. Harmoniae participem diximus, quia ita effertur, ut possit etiam cum alio sono ad harmoniam concinnari.*

A note cannot be divided, like a point in geometry. It is said to partake of harmony because it can be sounded so as to fit with another note to form harmony.

- **Russian base** · D. Clearly distinguishes the analogy and the reason for harmony participation.
- **English base** · B = D. Give idiomatic expression to the capacity to be fitted to another note.

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Clarify that the analogy is with the indivisibility of a single pitch. Euclid, Elements I, definition 1 [from memory].

### X13 · Xyl. 41.13–16

*Quod nisi fiat, iam is dissonus sit sonus, non consonus: imo neque sonus dicetur esse, sed strepitus quispiam temere editus, et ad consonantiam nulli existens usui.*

If this does not occur, the sound is dissonant rather than consonant. It is then classed as randomly produced noise that is of no use for consonance.

- **Russian base** · D. Keeps the successive qualifications and reads naturally.
- **English base** · A = D. Preserve the two adjectives and the demotion from note to noise.

**Errors**

- **B · en · minor mistranslation** · “the note will be dissonant, with no consonance”. Non consonus predicates the opposite adjective of the note and pairs it with dissonus. The paraphrase casts an abstract property as an added circumstance. **Fix** · the note will be dissonant, not consonant *(confirmed)*

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · The contrast of strepitus with sonus explains why noise falls outside the definition in X08. Compare Boethius, De institutione musica I.8, ed. Friedlein (1867), p. 195 [checked]

### X14 · Xyl. 41.16–25

*Proinde diversae sunt sonorum, quas illi progressu et ordine, quem a gravi ad acutum observant, nanciscuntur nomenclationes: Proslambanomenos, Hypate hypaton, parhypate hypaton, hypate diatonos, hypate meson, parhypate meson, diatonos meson, mese monos, paramesos, trite diezeugmene, diatonos diezeugmene, nete diezeugmene, trite hyperbolaeon, diatonos hyperbolaeon, nete hyperbolaeon.*

Notes receive their names according to the order in which they advance from low to high. The list has fifteen positions in the disjunct two-octave system, including mese monos and Xylander's paramesos.

- **Russian base** · D. Gives the ascending sequence and all the unusual nomenclature, with a clear contextual rendering of monos.
- **English base** · C. Keeps the entire series, including the unusual literal label mese monos, and explains it.

**Divergence · mese monos**

Is monos retained as part of the name or translated as a qualifier?

- **A** · Retains the Greek modifier as an integral element of the label in both languages.
  - A · RU «меса монос» / EN “mese monos”
- **B, D** · Explains the word adverbially as simply mese in both languages.
  - B · RU «просто меса» / EN “simply mese”
  - D · RU «просто меса» / EN “simply mese”
- **C** · Makes it an adjectival qualifier in Russian but retains the Greek form in English.
  - C · RU «меса единственная» / EN “mese monos”

**Verdict** · Monos is printed inside the list. Its masculine ending is anomalous next to feminine mese. Vinet's independent Latin wording Mese solum supports a modifier meaning alone or simply, with no change to the number of notes. Retaining the form with a note best meets the convention to preserve names as printed.

- **Russian** · меса монос
- **English** · mese monos
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.22 [checked] · LSJ, s.v. μόνος I [checked] · Vinet, Tournon (1591), supplied reading text V17 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.22, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 213–215 [checked] · about.md §4.4 [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · medium · confirmed · for the editor

**Errors**

- **A · en · minor language** · “the names of the notes are various, which they acquire”. The detached relative clause makes it awkward to recover names as the object of acquire and notes as the subject. **Fix** · the notes acquire different names according to their progression and order *(confirmed)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **A · doubt · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · tiam nulli existens usui. Proinde diuersæ sunt / sonorum, quas illi progressu et ordine, quem à / graui ad acutum obseruant, nanciscuntur no- / menclationes: Proslambanomenos, Hypate hy- / paton, parhypate hypaton, hypate diatonos, hy- / pate meson, parhypate meson, diatonos meson, / mese monos, paramesos, trite diezeugmene, dia- / tonos diezeugmene, nete diezeugmene, trite / hyperbolæon, diatonos hyperbolæon, nete hy- / perbolæon. Per has sonorum species heccæde
  - **Proposal** · меса одиночная / the single mese
  - The adjective single conveys the ordinary lexical sense. As a label it can imply uniqueness of this pitch or context rather than the absence of a further designation.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.22 [checked] · LSJ, s.v. μόνος I [checked] · Vinet, supplied reading text V17 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.22, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 213–215 [checked]
- **B · doubt · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · tiam nulli existens usui. Proinde diuersæ sunt / sonorum, quas illi progressu et ordine, quem à / graui ad acutum obseruant, nanciscuntur no- / menclationes: Proslambanomenos, Hypate hy- / paton, parhypate hypaton, hypate diatonos, hy- / pate meson, parhypate meson, diatonos meson, / mese monos, paramesos, trite diezeugmene, dia- / tonos diezeugmene, nete diezeugmene, trite / hyperbolæon, diatonos hyperbolæon, nete hy- / perbolæon. Per has sonorum species heccæde
  - **Proposal** · одна лишь меса / mese alone
  - The lexical gloss is defensible, but could be read as excluding other notes in a context that enumerates them. Vinet's solum supports taking it as a qualification of the name.

Proinde diuersæ sunt / sonorum, quas illi progressu et ordine, quem à / graui ad acutum obseruant, nanciscuntur no- / menclationes: Proslambanomenos, Hypate hy- / paton, parhypate hypaton, hypate diatonos, hy- / pate meson, parhypate meson, diatonos meson, / mese monos, paramesos, trite diezeugmene, dia- / tonos diezeugmene, nete diezeugmene, trite / hyperbolæon, diatonos hyperbolæon, nete hy- / perbolæon. Per has sonorum species heccæde

- **A** · Note the fifteen positions, the missing conjunct alternatives, and the anomalous monos beside feminine mese.

**Editor questions**

- Decide whether to retain monos as part of the printed name in both languages or gloss it in the running text. Give its sense in a note either way.

### X15 · Xyl. 41.25–42.5

*Per has sonorum species heccaedecachordum, hoc est, sedecim chordarum instrumentum in quindecim fidibus instructo organo (Pentecaedecachordum vocant Graeci) absolvitur, fine octachordi ad principium sequentium continuo assumpto.*

Xylander says that a sixteen-string arrangement is realized on an instrument actually equipped with fifteen strings. The end of the first octachord serves immediately as the beginning of the following series. Two eight-note spans therefore share one position, giving 8 + 8 − 1 = 15.

- **Russian base** · A. Preserves the two formations and avoids saying there is a physical instrument inside another.
- **English base** · C. Preserves both formations, the shared endpoint and the explicitly bracketed noun recovered from context.

**Divergence · heccaedecachordum … in quindecim fidibus instructo organo … absolvitur**

How is the sixteen-string entity related to the instrument of fifteen strings?

- **A, C** · A named sixteen-string entity is realized on the physically fifteen-string instrument, with both names retained as Greek-derived forms.
  - A · RU «Этими видами звуков геккайдекахорд, то есть инструмент о шестнадцати струнах, осуществляется на инструменте, снабжённом пятнадцатью струнами (греки называют его пентекайдекахордом), поскольку конец октахорда тотчас принимается за начало последующих.» / EN “Through these species of notes the hekkaidekachord, that is, the instrument of sixteen strings, is completed on an instrument furnished with fifteen strings (the Greeks call it a pentekaidekachord), the end of the octachord being taken up directly as the beginning of those that follow.”
  - C · RU «Посредством этих видов звуков геккайдекахорд, то есть инструмент из шестнадцати струн, осуществляется на инструменте, снабжённом пятнадцатью струнами (греки называют его пентекайдекахордом): конец октахорда сразу же принимается за начало последующих [звуков].» / EN “Through these species of notes the hekkaidekachord, that is, an instrument of sixteen strings, is completed on an instrument fitted with fifteen strings (the Greeks call it the pentekaidekachord), the end of the octachord being taken immediately as the beginning of the following [notes].”
- **B** · The sixteen-string system, glossed as an instrument, is described as inside another instrument.
  - B · RU «Эти виды звуков образуют шестнадцатиструнную систему, то есть шестнадцатиструнный инструмент в инструменте, снабжённом пятнадцатью струнами (греки называют его пятнадцатиструнником): конец октахорда сразу принимают за начало следующих [звуков].» / EN “These kinds of notes complete a sixteen-string system, that is, an instrument of sixteen strings within an instrument equipped with fifteen strings (the Greeks call it a fifteen-string instrument), the end of the octachord being taken immediately as the beginning of the [notes] that follow.”
- **D** · The forms are translated descriptively. The sixteen-string arrangement is realized on a fifteen-string instrument.
  - D · RU «Из этих видов звуков на инструменте с пятнадцатью струнами (греки называют его пятнадцатиструнным) складывается шестнадцатиструнный [инструмент], то есть инструмент с шестнадцатью струнами. При этом конец октахорда непосредственно принимается за начало следующих [звуков].» / EN “On an instrument strung with fifteen strings (the Greeks call it the fifteen-stringed instrument), these kinds of notes complete the sixteen-stringed instrument, that is, an instrument of sixteen strings. The end of the octachord is taken straightaway as the beginning of the [notes] that follow.”

**Verdict** · In this context the ablative with in marks the instrument on which the arrangement is completed. The sixteen named positions count the boundary note twice. The enclosure wording can mislead. Describing the compounds is sound if their form is given in the note.

- **Russian** · На инструменте с пятнадцатью струнами (греки называют его пентекайдекахордом) осуществляется геккайдекахорд, то есть инструмент с шестнадцатью струнами.
- **English** · The hekkaidekachord, that is, the instrument of sixteen strings, is realized on an instrument equipped with fifteen strings, which the Greeks call a pentekaidekachord.
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 41.25–42.7 [checked] · Allen and Greenough, New Latin Grammar §426.3 on the place where, in with ablative [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.24–25, ed. Friedlein (1867), shared boundary and disjunction of tetrachords [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · modified · for the editor

**Errors**

- **B · both · major construal** · “шестнадцатиструнный инструмент в инструменте / an instrument of sixteen strings within an instrument”. Within suggests that one physical instrument contains another. The text contrasts the number of places in the arrangement with the number of actual strings on which it is played. **Fix** · на инструменте … / on an instrument … *(confirmed)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **B · doubt · unsound** (confirmed)
  - **Printed** · perbolæon. Per has sonorum species heccæde / cachordum, hoc est, sedecim chordarum instru- / mentum in quindecim fidibus instructo organo / (Pentecædecachordum uocant Græci) absol- / uitur, fine octachordi ad principium sequentium / continuò assumpto. Quam enim rationem ha-
  - **Proposal** · конец октахорда сразу принимают за начало следующих [октахордов] / the end of the octachord being taken immediately as the beginning of the [octachords] that follow
  - The alternative supplies a plural octachords when the calculation requires only two in all, one preceding and one following. The plural participle is easier as the notes or strings that follow. A rendering without an expressed head would retain the small ambiguity.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 42.4–7 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. continuus, adverb continuo [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Keep the two counts and the distinction among chorda, fides and nervus. Explain that the two spans share the eighth note.
- **D** · Keep the distinction between named places and actual strings.

**Editor questions**

- Choose whether to give the unusual Greek-derived forms in the running text or to translate their transparent meanings and record the forms in a note.

### X16 · Xyl. 42.5–7

*Quam enim rationem habet primus sonus ad octavum, eandem octavus ad decimumquintum obtinet.*

The first note stands to the eighth in the same ratio as the eighth to the fifteenth. Each ascending span is an octave. The pitch multiplier is 2 in each case, 4 for the whole span. The frequency ratio in the order of the Latin is 1:2 for each span, and the inverse direction applies to string lengths at fixed tension and density.

- **Russian base** · B = D. Both state the two ordered comparisons idiomatically.
- **English base** · B = D. Both keep the ratios and their endpoints clear.

**Errors**

- **A · notes · minor note-claim** · “1st : 8th = octave, 2:1; 8th : 15th = octave, 2:1; 1st : 15th = 4:1”. The checks write the lower note first but label each pair with the conventional higher-over-lower pitch ratio without identifying it as such. In the written order the frequency ratios are 1:2, 1:2 and 1:4. An ideal string-length interpretation would invert those. **Fix** · State that the ascending pitch multipliers are 2, 2 and 4, and specify the order whenever assigning pitch or string-length ratios. *(added)*
- **C · notes · minor note-claim** · “1 (proslambanomenos) : 8 (mese) = 8 (mese) : 15 (nete hyperbolaion) = an octave, 2:1 in each case; 1 : 15 = double octave, 4:1”. The checks write the lower note first but label each pair with the conventional higher-over-lower pitch ratio without identifying it as such. In the written order the frequency ratios are 1:2, 1:2 and 1:4. An ideal string-length interpretation would invert those. **Fix** · State that the ascending pitch multipliers are 2, 2 and 4, and specify the order whenever assigning pitch or string-length ratios. *(added)*

**Notes to keep**

- **B** · Keep the distinction between an ascending pitch multiplier and a frequency ratio written in the order of the Latin terms.

### X17 · Xyl. 42.7

*Et haec de sonis.*

This concludes the account of notes.

- **Russian base** · C = D. Both give the brief conventional transition.
- **English base** · A = B = C = D. All render the closing formula idiomatically.

### X18 · Xyl. 42.8–15

*Intervallorum autem minimum genus est, quod diesin vocant: deinde semitonium, cuius dupla ad acutum est accessio, respectu dieseos. Deinde est tonus duplici constans semitonio, et rationem sesquioctavam constituens, ut est novenarii ad octonarium numerum. Quo fit, ut semitonium sit quasi dimidium sesquioctavae diesis autem quasi quadrans eiusdem.*

The smallest interval is a diesis. Next comes the semitone, whose rise is double that of a diesis, then the tone, made of two semitones and standing in the sesquioctave ratio 9:8. The semitone is as it were half of that ratio and the diesis as it were a quarter. If the interval is divided in exactly equal musical steps, the resulting ratios are (9/8)^(1/2) and (9/8)^(1/4), approximately 1.06066 and 1.02988.

- **Russian base** · D. Preserves both occurrences of quasi and makes accessio intelligible in terms of pitch.
- **English base** · D. Clear scope for double and exact notation for the named ratio.

**Errors**

- **B · en · minor omission** · “and the diesis a quarter of it”. The Latin repeats quasi for the diesis. The English allows the second subdivision to be read as unqualified. **Fix** · and the diesis, as it were, a quarter of it *(confirmed)*
- **C · both · minor language** · “из двойного полутона / of a double semitone”. The expression can suggest one recognized kind of semitone called a double semitone. The text uses ordinary doubling to give the composition of the tone. **Fix** · из двух полутонов / of two semitones *(confirmed)*
- **A · notes · minor note-claim** · “neither is a ratio of whole numbers, hence the text's ‘quasi’”. The arithmetic is correct if the musical interval is split into equal logarithmic steps. The specific causal claim about Xylander's quasi is not verified. The word qualifies the analogy to a half or a quarter, and the text never states this modern calculation. **Fix** · Give the calculation conditionally and leave the force of quasi at ‘as it were’. Specify whole-number terms when contrasting Boethius's impossibility result with the subsequent geometric argument. *(added)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **A · text issue · sound** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · dimidium sesquioctauæ diesis autem (42.14)
  - **Proposal** · punctuate: dimidium sesquioctavae, diesis autem
  - The scan has no stop after sesquioctavae. The conjunction autem and the second quasi introduce a separate coordinated proposition. A comma or stronger point would make the already recoverable grammar clear.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 42.14 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. quasi I [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **B** · Distinguish dividing the numerical excess linearly from dividing the interval into equal multiplicative steps. Boethius's worked example uses 16, 17 and 18. Boethius, De institutione musica I.16–17, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 201–204 [checked]
- **D** · The rise is double in interval size, and both subdivisions are qualified by quasi.

**Editor questions**

- Insert punctuation between the two quasi clauses in the reading text if the normalization permits it.

### X19 · Xyl. 42.15–18

*Quamvis enim ratione numeri unitas dividi non potest, utique tamen unitas quae in materia conspicitur, dividitur Geometrice.*

In arithmetic, the unit cannot be divided. A unit discerned in material magnitude can nonetheless be divided geometrically.

- **Russian base** · A. Distinguishes arithmetical reckoning from the material case without identifying the unit as a number.
- **English base** · A. The unit is the term used in the mathematical distinction.

**Divergence · ratione numeri unitas dividi non potest**

Does ratione numeri characterize the domain or make the unit a number?

- **A, C** · The unit is indivisible under the rules of number.
  - A · RU «Ведь хотя с точки зрения числа единица делиться не может, всё же единица, усматриваемая в материи, делится геометрически.» / EN “For although in terms of number the unit cannot be divided, yet the unit that is seen in matter is divided geometrically.”
  - C · RU «Ибо хотя с точки зрения числа единица не может быть разделена, однако единица, которая усматривается в материи, безусловно делится геометрически.» / EN “For although, by the reckoning of number, unity cannot be divided, yet unity that is seen in matter is certainly divided geometrically.”
- **B, D** · B in both languages and D in Russian describe the unit as a number. D English instead says ‘considered numerically’, which preserves the domain distinction.
  - B · RU «Ведь хотя единица как число неделима, единица, которую мы наблюдаем в материи, вполне допускает геометрическое деление.» / EN “For although unity cannot be divided as a number, unity as observed in matter can indeed be divided geometrically.”
  - D · RU «Ведь хотя единица, если рассматривать её как число, не поддаётся делению, единицу, данную в материи, всё же можно разделить геометрически.» / EN “For although the unit cannot be divided when considered numerically, the unit as discerned in matter can certainly be divided geometrically.”

**Verdict** · Ratione numeri locates the statement within number or numerical reasoning. A/C and D English retain that distinction. B and D Russian are intelligible but risk implying that the unit is itself a number. Euclid expressly distinguishes a unit from a number, which is a multitude of units.

- **Russian** · Хотя в арифметическом счёте единица неделима, единицу, данную в материи, всё же можно разделить геометрически.
- **English** · Although the unit is indivisible in arithmetic, the unit discerned in matter can nevertheless be divided geometrically.
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 42.15–18 [checked] · Euclid, Elements VII, definitions 1–2 [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · modified

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Explain the contrast between the unit that grounds counting and continuously divisible magnitude. Euclid, Elements VII, definitions 1–2 [checked].

### X20 · Xyl. 42.18–21

*Nam chordam eam quae observat rationem sesquioctavam, possibile est dividi in semisesquioctavam, quod est semitonium, et hoc denuo dividi in diesin.*

The string corresponding to a sesquioctave ratio 9:8 can be divided to yield the semisesquioctave (the semitone), and that can again be divided to yield a diesis. Equal division of the tone would use the mean proportional of the two bounding lengths, such as √72 between 8 and 9.

- **Russian base** · D. Clearly marks the two successive results and retains the ratio name.
- **English base** · B = D. Yield makes the division intelligible without taking the resulting interval for a physical string segment.

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Explain the geometric mean and distinguish a midpoint of string length from the point giving equal intervals. Euclid, Elements VI.13, Clark University edition [checked]

### X21 · Xyl. 42.21–25

*Geometriae enim est, semper a maiori recta minorem posse detrahere, semperque posse minori apponere maiorem. ex quo sequitur, ut quaelibet figura ad placitum possit cum augeri, tum diminui.*

Geometry can always subtract the lesser straight length from the greater and add the greater to the lesser. A figure can accordingly be enlarged or diminished at will.

- **Russian base** · D. Отрезок прямой correctly gives the finite straight magnitude under discussion.
- **English base** · D. Shorter and longer are exact in the geometrical setting, and each semper is kept.

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Euclid's construction of removing the equivalent of a lesser straight line from a greater supplies the mathematical parallel. Euclid, Elements I.3 [checked].

### X22 · Xyl. 42.25–43.1

*Id vero in Arithmetica locum non habet.*

The geometrical freedom just described does not hold in arithmetic.

- **Russian base** · D. Carries the argument forward naturally.
- **English base** · D. Gives the scope of id and the contrast signaled by vero.

### X23 · Xyl. 43.1–10

*Ad haec, propositum quadratum in aequalia partiri quadrata, aut duplum efficere non potest. Numerorum enim sedecim, qui est quadratus, neque in duo potest diducere quadrata (octo enim Arithmetice quadratum esse, aut eius numeri dare radicem quadratam, impossibile est) neque duplum eius aliud quadratum dare (fieri enim non potest, ut numerus triginta duo sit quadratus) At Geometriae hoc factu non est difficile.*

Arithmetic cannot bisect a square number into two equal square numbers or double it and still have a square. Xylander illustrates this with sixteen, half of which is eight and double of which is thirty-two. Neither eight nor thirty-two is an integer square. Geometry can make both areas as squares. The transmitted Numerorum is syntactically awkward.

- **Russian base** · B. Keeps the mathematical point explicit and marks the repeated qualification equal as supplied.
- **English base** · B. Makes the two operations and their restriction to arithmetic clear.

**Divergence · Numerorum enim sedecim, qui est quadratus**

How should the irregular opening of the numerical example be construed?

- **A** · Construes numerorum as a loose partitive, explicitly retained as among numbers.
  - A · RU «Кроме того, [арифметика] не может разделить предложенный квадрат на равные квадраты или построить вдвое больший. Ведь среди чисел шестнадцать, которое есть квадрат, она не может ни разложить на два квадрата (ибо арифметически невозможно, чтобы восемь было квадратом, и невозможно дать квадратный корень этого числа), ни дать другой квадрат, вдвое больший его (ибо не может быть, чтобы число тридцать два было квадратом). А для геометрии сделать это нетрудно.» / EN “Moreover, [arithmetic] cannot divide a given square into equal squares, or produce its double. For among numbers sixteen, which is a square, it can neither part into two squares (for it is impossible arithmetically for eight to be a square, or to give the square root of that number), nor can it give another square that is its double (for it cannot be that the number thirty-two is a square). But for geometry this is not hard to do.”
- **B, C, D** · Uses the intended numeral with an understood singular number rather than reproducing the loose genitive on its own.
  - B · RU «Кроме того, данный квадрат нельзя разделить на равные квадраты или получить из него квадрат вдвое больший. Ведь число шестнадцать — квадратное, но его нельзя ни разложить на два [равных] квадрата (в арифметике число восемь не может быть квадратом, и квадратный корень из него дать невозможно), ни удвоить так, чтобы получить другой квадрат (число тридцать два не может быть квадратным). В геометрии же сделать это нетрудно.» / EN “Moreover, a given square cannot be divided into equal squares or made into a square twice its size. For one cannot split the number sixteen, which is square, into two [equal] squares (in arithmetic eight cannot be a square, nor can a square root be given for that number), or double it to give another square (for thirty-two cannot be a square number). In geometry this is not difficult to do.”
  - C · RU «Кроме того, [арифметика] не может ни разделить предложенный квадрат на равные квадраты, ни сделать его двойным. Ведь число шестнадцать, которое является квадратом, она не может ни разложить на два квадрата (ибо невозможно ни восьми быть арифметически квадратом, ни дать квадратный корень этого числа), ни дать другой квадрат, вдвое больший его (ибо не может быть, чтобы число тридцать два было квадратом). Геометрии же сделать это нетрудно.» / EN “Moreover, [arithmetic] cannot divide a proposed square into equal squares, or make it double. For it cannot split the number sixteen, which is a square, into two squares (for it is impossible for eight to be arithmetically a square, or to give the square root of that number), nor give another square double of it (for it cannot be that the number thirty-two is a square). But for geometry this is not difficult to do.”
  - D · RU «Кроме того, арифметика не может ни разделить данный квадрат на равные квадраты, ни удвоить его. Таково, например, квадратное число шестнадцать. Арифметика не может ни разложить его на два квадрата (ведь невозможно, чтобы восемь было квадратом в арифметическом смысле или чтобы можно было указать квадратный корень этого числа), ни получить другой квадрат, удвоив шестнадцать (ведь число тридцать два не может быть квадратом). Геометрии же сделать это нетрудно.» / EN “Moreover, arithmetic cannot divide a given square into equal squares or double it. Take the number sixteen, which is a square. Arithmetic can neither resolve it into two squares (for eight cannot be a square in arithmetic, nor can one give a square root of that number) nor give its double as another square (for the number thirty-two cannot be a square). Geometry, however, finds no difficulty in doing this.”

**Verdict** · The singular relative leads to numerus as the implied term, and sixteen is what arithmetic tries to divide. A's construction makes the print's difficulty visible, but among numbers sixteen can sound like sixteen unspecified numbers. Translate the one numeral clearly and put the loose genitive and possible emendation in the commentary.

- **Russian** · Ведь число шестнадцать, будучи квадратным, нельзя разделить на два квадрата…
- **English** · For arithmetic cannot divide sixteen, which is a square number, into two squares…
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.1–10 and marginal diagrams [checked] · Allen and Greenough, New Latin Grammar §§345–346 [checked] · Euclid, Elements VII, definitions 1–2 [checked] · Euclid, Elements VII, definition 18, on the square number [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · modified · for the editor

**Errors**

- **D · both · minor sign** · “arithmetic cannot divide / арифметика не может ни разделить”. D supplies an explicit subject from the preceding segment. That is a sound interpretation, but the project's sign convention requires marking the supplied subject. **Fix** · Use ‘[arithmetic] cannot divide’ / «[арифметика] не может разделить», or use an idiomatic impersonal construction. *(added)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **A · text issue · sound** (confirmed · for the editor)

### X24 · Xyl. 43.10–14

*nam Geometer dati quadrati latere loco diametri accepto, ubi circumposuit quadratum, id statim propositi est dimidium: iterumque accepta dati quadrati diametro loco lateris, ex eo quadratum describet, quod propositum dupli esse constat.*

Taking the original side as the new diagonal gives a square of half the original area. Taking the original diagonal as the new side gives a square of twice the original area. For side s, the areas are s²/2 and 2s². The ending propositum dupli is irregular, though its result is fully determined by the construction.

- **Russian base** · B. Clearly states both geometrical operations and their direction.
- **English base** · B. Gives the two areas with an unambiguous comparison to the original.

**Divergence · quod propositum dupli esse constat**

Is the result another square twice the original or the original square in a doubled size?

- **A, B** · The new square is twice the original.
  - A · RU «Ибо стоит геометру, приняв сторону данного квадрата за диагональ, построить вокруг [неё] квадрат, как тот сразу оказывается половиной предложенного; и опять-таки, приняв диагональ данного квадрата за сторону, он начертит на ней квадрат, который, как известно, вдвое больше предложенного.» / EN “For the geometer, having taken the side of the given square in place of the diagonal, has only to set a square about [it], and that is at once the half of the one proposed; and again, taking the diagonal of the given square in place of the side, he will describe on it a square which is known to be the double of the one proposed.”
  - B · RU «В самом деле, если геометр возьмёт сторону данного квадрата за диагональ и построит вокруг неё квадрат, то сразу получит половину данного квадрата. Если же он примет диагональ данного квадрата за сторону, то построит на ней квадрат, который, как ясно, вдвое больше данного.» / EN “For when a geometer takes the side of the given square as a diagonal and constructs a square around it, the new square is at once half the given one. Conversely, he will take the diagonal of the given square as a side and construct on it a square evidently twice the size of the original.”
- **C** · The new square is the original given in doubled size, preserving the surface cases through a genitive of measure.
  - C · RU «Ведь геометр, приняв сторону данного квадрата вместо диагонали, — едва он опишет вокруг [неё] квадрат, этот [квадрат] тотчас оказывается половиной предложенного; и опять, приняв диагональ данного квадрата вместо стороны, он опишет на ней квадрат, который, как известно, есть предложенный [квадрат] в двойном размере.» / EN “For the geometer, having taken the side of the given square in place of a diagonal, as soon as he has drawn a square around [it], that [square] is at once half of the proposed one; and again, having taken the diagonal of the given square in place of a side, he will describe a square on it, which, as is evident, is the proposed [square] of double [size].”
- **D** · The new square effects the envisaged doubling without explicitly stating the two terms of the comparison.
  - D · RU «В самом деле, если геометр возьмёт сторону данного квадрата за диагональ и опишет вокруг неё квадрат, то сразу получит половину исходного квадрата. Если же он, наоборот, примет диагональ данного квадрата за сторону, то опишет на ней квадрат и тем самым, очевидно, получит искомое удвоение.» / EN “For if the geometer takes the side of the given square as a diagonal and constructs a square about it, the result is at once half the original. If in turn he takes the diagonal of the given square as a side, he will draw on it a square which clearly gives the doubling proposed.”

**Verdict** · The intended mathematical comparison is beyond doubt: the square built on the original diagonal has twice the original area. The printed endings cannot easily give that comparative idiom. A and B are clearest about which square has which area. C usefully draws attention to the transmitted difficulty, but its wording can suggest the same square changes size.

- **Russian** · …квадрат, который, как ясно, вдвое больше данного.
- **English** · …a square which is clearly twice the area of the original.
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.10–14 [checked] · Allen and Greenough, New Latin Grammar §§345–346 [checked] · Euclid, Elements I.47, Clark University edition [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · confirmed · for the editor

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **A · text issue · sound** (modified · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · quod propositū dupli esse cōstat (43.14; confirmed on the page image)
  - **Proposal** · quod propositi duplum esse constat
  - The scan has abbreviated propositum, formally nominative or accusative neuter followed by dupli. Exchanging the endings gives a partitive expression exactly parallel to propositi … dimidium and fits the geometrical construction. The transmitted text still communicates the result in context, so the choice to emend remains editorial.

  - **Proposal** · …он опишет на ней квадрат, который, как известно, вдвое больше предложенного. / …he will describe a square on it, which is evidently double the proposed one.

  - **Proposal** · который, как известно, вдвое больше исходного квадрата / which is known to be twice the original square
  - The rendering correctly identifies the new square as twice the area of the original. The conclusion is secured by the construction and the explicit discussion in X23. The question of adopting the corresponding alteration of the Latin endings is separate.

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Explain that diametrum denotes the diagonal of a square. The second construction also occurs in Plato, Meno 82b–85b [from memory].
- **D** · Retain the explanation that the new square areas follow from Euclid's theorem, with no integral side length required. Euclid, Elements I.47, Clark University edition [checked]

**Editor questions**

- The arithmetic and the first clause settle the direction of the comparison. Decide whether to keep the transmitted propositum dupli with a note or adopt propositi duplum.

### X25 · Xyl. 43.15–16

*Sic etiam sesquioctava toni proportio dividi poterit in semitonium, iterumque in diesin.*

By the same geometrical logic, the sesquioctave 9:8 proportion of the tone can be divided to yield a semitone, and divided again to yield a diesis.

- **Russian base** · B. It is clear what the two divisions yield, and proportio remains distinguishable from ratio.
- **English base** · B. Preserves the characteristic word proportio, the bracket and the successive divisions.

### X26 · Xyl. 43.16–21

*Eorundem quoque auctione, compositae quoque reliquae intervallorum species constituuntur: tridiesis, trisemitonium, ditonus, tritonus, et quo quis modo voluerit, idque rhythmus admiserit, veluti ex sequentibus cognoscetur.*

Increasing the same intervals produces the remaining composite kinds: tridiesis, trihemitone, ditone, tritone, and such other increases as one wishes and rhythm allows. The stated sizes are ¾, 1½, 2 and 3 tones respectively. The printer reads compositae. The next sentence calls these intervals incomposite when they are delivered in a single step.

- **Russian base** · A. Keeps the printed category in a well-formed clause and shows the supplement in brackets.
- **English base** · A = B. Both keep the printed category and the limit of admissibility set by rhythm.

**Divergence · et quo quis modo voluerit, idque rhythmus admiserit**

Do we have a qualifier on further magnitudes or a second independent condition?

- **A, C, D** · Retains the parallel expressions of choice and what rhythm admits in both languages.
  - A · RU «Приращением их же образуются и прочие, составные, виды интервалов: тридиеса, трёхполутон, дитон, тритон и [иные] — как кто пожелает и как допустит ритм, что станет понятно из дальнейшего.» / EN “By the increase of these same, the remaining, composite species of intervals are also constituted: the tridiesis, the trihemitone, the ditone, the tritone, and [others] in whatever way one may wish and rhythm allow, as will be learned from what follows.”
  - C · RU «Их же наращиванием образуются и остальные виды интервалов, также составные: тридиеса, трёхполутон, дитон, тритон — и каким кто ни пожелает образом и [насколько] это допустит ритм, как станет ясно из последующего.» / EN “By the augmentation of the same the remaining species of intervals, likewise composite, are also constituted: the tridiesis, the trihemitone, the ditone, the tritone, and in whatever way anyone wishes and the rhythm admits it, as will be learned from what follows.”
  - D · RU «При увеличении этих же [интервалов] образуются и остальные виды интервалов — составные: тридиеса, трёхполутон, дитон, тритон, а также [другие], какие угодно и какие допустит ритм, как будет видно из дальнейшего.» / EN “By increasing these same [intervals] one also forms the other, composite kinds of intervals: the tridiesis, the trihemitone, the ditone, the tritone and [others] in whatever manner one wishes and the rhythm allows, as will appear from what follows.”
- **B** · Expresses rhythm's permission as an explicit condition in Russian, while retaining coordination in English.
  - B · RU «Увеличением тех же [интервалов] образуются и остальные составные виды интервала: тридиеса, трёхполутон, дитон, тритон — и как угодно ещё, если это допускает ритм, как будет видно из дальнейшего.» / EN “By increasing these same [intervals] the remaining composite kinds of interval are also formed: the tridiesis, trihemitone, ditone, tritone, and whatever else one wishes and rhythm allows, as will be seen from what follows.”

**Verdict** · Idque refers to the manner chosen. It adds rhythm's allowance as a limit on choice, so a well-formed conditional paraphrase is possible. Preserving the coordination or using an as-phrase gives the same practical two criteria.

- **Russian** · и как кто пожелает, если это допустит ритм
- **English** · and in whatever manner one wishes and rhythm allows
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.16–21 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. auctio I [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · medium · confirmed

**Errors**

- **C · en · minor language** · “in whatever way anyone wishes and the rhythm admits it”. The final it leaves a coordinated independent clause attached to the subordinate 'in whatever way'. **Fix** · in whatever manner one wishes and rhythm allows *(confirmed)*

**Text proposals and doubts**

- **B · text issue · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · compositæ quoque reliquæ inter- / uallorum species
  - **Proposal** · Possibly incompositae quoque reliquae intervallorum species
  - The scan clearly reads compositae. The reading incompositae matches the label in X27 but removes the printed tension, which admits a coherent distinction between size assembled by increase and performance in one step. There is no demonstrated textual need for the emendation.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.17–24 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked] · Vinet, supplied reading text V31–V32 [checked]
- **C · text issue · sound** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · quoque auctione, compositæ quoque reliquæ in-
  - **Proposal** · Keep compositae; an emendation incompositae would be possible but is not necessary.
  - Keep compositae. The word is clear on the scan, and the next sentence can explain in what contrasting sense the same sizes are called incomposite. Replacing it with incompositae would remove this possible change of perspective.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.17–24 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked] · Vinet, supplied reading text V31–V32 [checked]
- **B · doubt · open** (confirmed · for the editor)
  - **Printed** · in semitonium, iterúmque in diesin. Eorundem / quoque auctione, compositæ quoque reliquæ in- / teruallorum species constituuntur: tridiesis, tri- / semitonium, ditonus, tritonus, et quo quis mo- / do uoluerit, idqúe rhythmus admiserit, ueluti ex / sequentibus cognoscetur. Incomposita autem
  - **Proposal** · Увеличением тех же [интервалов] образуются и остальные несоставные виды интервала. / By increasing these same [intervals] the remaining incomposite kinds of interval are also formed.
  - The same doctrinal reservation applies to this alternative. It would be a rendering of a conjectural Latin text, not of compositae in the reading text.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.17–24 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked]
- **A · doubt · sound** (confirmed)
  - **Printed** · in semitonium, iterúmque in diesin. Eorundem / quoque auctione, compositæ quoque reliquæ in- / teruallorum species constituuntur: tridiesis, tri- / semitonium, ditonus, tritonus, et quo quis mo- / do uoluerit, idqúe rhythmus admiserit, ueluti ex / sequentibus cognoscetur. Incomposita autem
  - **Proposal** · и так, как кто пожелает, лишь бы это допустил ритм / and in whatever way one may wish, provided that rhythm allows it
  - It is legitimate to make the limit on what one wishes explicit as a condition. This preserves the referent of id and the ordinary sense of admittere.
  - **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.19–20 [checked]

**Notes to keep**

- **C** · Explain the semantic double perspective of composition by size and an incomposite step in performance. Boethius's chromatic and diatonic trihemitones supply a parallel. Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked]
- **D** · Retain rhythmus in the running text and explain that the text supplies no specification of the limit.

**Editor questions**

- Keep compositae in the translation while deciding how much weight should be given to the category alternation across X26–X27.

### X27 · Xyl. 43.21–44.1

*Incomposita autem dicuntur, quia tanquam una cantilena ex coalescentibus in unum tribus diesibus, aut semitoniis totidem, aut diversis tonis conflatur, potentia quidem sive facultate diversa intervalla continens, sed unico tamen intervallo edita.*

These are called incomposite because three dieses, as many semitones, or several tones coalesce in each case into what is like one tune. It contains the component intervals in potency and is uttered as one interval.

- **Russian base** · C. Several tones makes the quantity clear, and it keeps the comparison and the manner of performance.
- **English base** · C. Distinguishes several tones from different intervals and keeps the concessive relation.

**Divergence · aut diversis tonis**

Are the tones several in number or distinct in identity?

- **A, B, D** · Uses different, various or distinct tones, risking a reading as different kinds of tone.
  - A · RU «различных тонов» / EN “various tones”
  - B · RU «разных тонов» / EN “different tones”
  - D · RU «разных тонов» / EN “distinct tones”
- **C** · Several tones, referring to a multiplicity of tone units.
  - C · RU «нескольких тонов» / EN “several tones”

**Verdict** · The contrasting examples refer to the ditone and tritone of X26. The tones are combined as standard units. Vinet's multis also emphasizes quantity. Distinct components is possible, but the rendering should avoid implying different interval sizes.

- **Russian** · или из нескольких тонов
- **English** · or from several tones
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 43.21–44.1 [checked] · Lewis and Short, A Latin Dictionary, s.v. diverto, participial adjective diversus [checked] · Vinet, supplied reading text V31–V32 [checked] · Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · modified

**Errors**

- **A · notes · minor note-claim** · “‘different tones’ = ditone (2) or tritone (3)”. The preceding list supplies two and three tones as examples, but X27 does not specify their count and X26 explicitly allows further sizes as permitted by rhythm. **Fix** · Treat the ditone and tritone as examples of the unspecified group of several tones. *(added)*
- **C · notes · minor note-claim** · “several tones = ditone, tritone”. The preceding list supplies two and three tones as examples, but X27 does not specify their count and X26 explicitly allows further sizes as permitted by rhythm. **Fix** · Treat the ditone and tritone as examples of the unspecified group of several tones. *(added)*

**Notes to keep**

- **D** · Explain the one-step criterion by Boethius's chromatic trihemitone and the two-step diatonic counterpart. Boethius, De institutione musica I.23, ed. Friedlein (1867) [checked]
- **C** · Keep the contrast of Xylander's cantilena with Vinet's melos and the gloss of potentia by facultas.

### X28 · Xyl. 44.2–5

*Praeter haec sunt et alia intervalla: diatessaron, quartam vulgo dicunt: diapente, iisdem quinta est: et diapason, quam trito vocabulo octavam nominant.*

The text introduces three further intervals and pairs each Greek name with the ordinary Latin name quarta, quinta, or octava. The same people use these familiar labels.

- **Russian base** · A. Keeps all three double designations and their ordinary equivalents.
- **English base** · D. The literal gloss gives a recognizable counterpart to each ordinary name.

**Divergence · diatessaron, quartam … diapente, iisdem quinta … diapason … octavam**

How does the rendering preserve the paired Greek and Latin names?

- **A** · Uses literal Greek-name glosses and follows them with the accepted terms.
  - A · RU «Кроме них есть и другие интервалы: «через четыре», который в обиходе называют квартой; «через пять» — у них же это квинта; и «через все», который расхожим словом именуют октавой.» / EN “Besides these there are other intervals as well: the ‘through four’, which they commonly call the fourth; the ‘through five’, which for the same people is the fifth; and the ‘through all’, which they name by the well-worn word ‘octave’.”
- **B** · Uses standard names first and English or Russian ordinal glosses afterwards. English collapses the first two pairs into identical names.
  - B · RU «Есть и другие интервалы. Кварту обычно называют «четвёртой», квинту те же люди называют «пятой», а для октавы употребляют расхожее слово «восьмая».» / EN “There are other intervals besides these. The fourth and fifth go by those names in ordinary speech, and the octave is commonly called the “eighth.””
- **C** · Transliterates the Greek names and keeps the standard terms as their explicit Latin counterparts.
  - C · RU «Кроме них, есть и другие интервалы: диатессарон — в обиходе его называют квартой; диапенте — у тех же это квинта; и диапазон, который расхожим словом именуют октавой.» / EN “Besides these there are also other intervals: diatessaron, which is commonly called the fourth; diapente, which for the same people is the fifth; and diapason, which by a well-worn word they name the octave.”
- **D** · Russian gives the standard names with bracketed literal glosses, then the ordinals. English starts from the literal glosses and gives the standard names.
  - D · RU «Помимо этих интервалов есть и другие. Кварту [«через четыре»] в обиходе называют «четвёртой», квинту [«через пять»] те же люди называют «пятой», а октаву [«через все»], по привычному выражению, — «восьмой».» / EN “There are other intervals besides these. The interval ‘through four’ is commonly called the fourth, ‘through five’ is what the same people call the fifth, and ‘through all’ goes by the familiar name of octave.”

**Verdict** · The three pairs are substantively distinct in the source. B's Russian ordinal gloss is intelligible, but the English erases the contrast in the first two cases. C makes the source-language distinction transparent but leaves unfamiliar terms in translation. A and D's English present both the literal name and the established musical term, as the project's rule for naming explanations permits.

- **Russian** · Есть и другие интервалы: «через четыре» — в обиходе кварта, «через пять» — у тех же людей квинта, и «через все» — по привычному названию октава.
- **English** · There are other intervals as well: ‘through four’, commonly called the fourth, ‘through five’, which the same people call the fifth, and ‘through all’, commonly called the octave.
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 44.2–13 [checked] · about.md §§4.1–4.2 [checked] · LSJ, s.v. διά, with genitive [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · confirmed · for the editor

**Errors**

- **B · en · major omission** · “The fourth and fifth go by those names in ordinary speech”. Two pairs of names become one self-referential assertion. Iisdem, which explicitly links the users of the two common terms, also disappears. **Fix** · ‘through four’, commonly called the fourth, and ‘through five’, which the same people call the fifth *(confirmed)*

**Notes to keep**

- **A** · Explain the contrast between the Greek names and Xylander's current Latin ordinal designations.
- **D** · X29 explains the Greek names by their inclusive counts.

**Editor questions**

- Use a consistent way of pairing a literal gloss and the standard interval name at X28–X29. Decide whether to repeat the etymologies where X29 gives the explicit rationale.

### X29 · Xyl. 44.5–13

*Haec autem neque immediata sunt, neque incomposita, sed per media omnia dicta intervalla transeunt. Atque haec quidem intervalla dicuntur, ratione sonitus qui est a primo sono ad extremum: quantitate autem intermediorum sonorum huiusmodi appellationes invenerunt, diatessaron quidem, quia per quatuor sonos aut nervos transit: diapente, quia per quinque: diapason, quia per omnes, hoc est, per octo.*

These larger intervals are neither immediate nor incomposite, since they pass through the intervening intervals already described. Their character as intervals is defined by the sounding from first to last note. Their names derive from the notes traversed: four for the fourth, five for the fifth, and all eight for the octave. The counts include both bounding notes.

- **Russian base** · A. Keeps the paired negation, the sounding span and the literal Greek-name glosses with the established names.
- **English base** · D. Makes immediata clear and keeps both the count and the established terms.

**Divergence · diatessaron … diapente … diapason**

Do the three terms carry their literal naming argument in the running text?

- **A, D** · Keeps the standard terms and gives literal force in the text, though D glosses only the first explicitly.
  - A · RU «Они же не являются ни непосредственными, ни несоставными, но проходят через все названные интервалы как через промежуточные. И интервалами они называются по звучанию, которое простирается от первого звука до крайнего; а такие названия они получили по количеству промежуточных звуков: кварта [«через четыре»] — потому что проходит через четыре звука, или струны; квинта [«через пять»] — потому что через пять; октава [«через все»] — потому что через все, то есть через восемь.» / EN “These, however, are neither immediate nor incomposite, but pass through all the aforesaid intervals as intermediate ones. And they are called intervals, indeed, with respect to the sound that there is from the first note to the last; but from the quantity of the intermediate notes they have found names of this kind: the fourth [‘through four’], because it passes through four notes or strings; the fifth [‘through five’], because through five; the octave [‘through all’], because through all, that is, through eight.”
  - D · RU «У этих интервалов есть промежуточные [ступени], и сами они не принадлежат к числу несоставных, а проходят через все названные промежуточные интервалы. Интервалами их называют по звучанию от первого звука до последнего, а свои особые названия они получили по числу промежуточных звуков. Так, кварта [называется «через четыре»], потому что проходит через четыре звука или струны, квинта — через пять, октава — через все, то есть через восемь.» / EN “These intervals neither lack intermediate [steps] nor are they incomposite. They pass through all the intervening intervals mentioned above. They are called intervals in view of the sound from the first note to the last. They acquired their particular names from the number of intervening notes. The fourth [is called ‘through four’] because it passes through four notes or strings, the fifth because it passes through five, and the octave because it passes through all, that is, through eight.”
- **B** · Gives the standard terms alone and supplies the etymological gloss in the notes.
  - B · RU «Они же не непосредственны и не несоставны, поскольку проходят через все названные промежуточные интервалы. Интервалами они называются по звучанию от первого звука до последнего, а названия такого рода были придуманы по числу промежуточных звуков: кварта — потому что проходит через четыре звука или струны, квинта — через пять, октава — через все, то есть через восемь.» / EN “These intervals are neither immediate nor incomposite, for they pass through all the intermediate intervals just mentioned. They are called intervals with reference to the sound from the first note to the last. Their particular names were devised from the number of intervening notes: the fourth because it passes through four notes or strings, the fifth because it passes through five, and the octave because it passes through all of them, that is, through eight.”
- **C** · Retains the Greek-derived forms in the running text and supplies literal meanings in a note.
  - C · RU «Они же не являются ни непосредственными, ни несоставными, но проходят через все названные промежуточные интервалы. И интервалами они называются по звучанию, которое простирается от первого звука до последнего; а по количеству промежуточных звуков они получили такие наименования: диатессарон — потому что проходит через четыре звука, или струны; диапенте — потому что через пять; диапазон — потому что через все, то есть через восемь.» / EN “These are neither immediate nor incomposite, but pass through all the aforesaid intervals in between. And they are called intervals by reason of the sounding that runs from the first note to the last; but from the quantity of the intermediate notes they received names of this kind: diatessaron, because it passes through four notes or strings; diapente, because through five; diapason, because through all, that is, through eight.”

**Verdict** · All keep the counts. Because the argument depends on the names being intelligible point by point, giving the established term and literal gloss side by side is clearest. Both the rule for established forms and the allowance for naming explanations apply here. Strictly interior notes would number two, three and six, so the wording ‘intermediate notes’ must be preserved with a note about inclusive counting.

- **Russian** · кварта [«через четыре»] … квинта [«через пять»] … октава [«через все»]
- **English** · the fourth [‘through four’] … the fifth [‘through five’] … the octave [‘through all’]
- **Evidence** · Xylander, Basel (1556), supplied scan 44.5–13 [checked] · about.md §§4.1–4.2 [checked] · LSJ, s.v. διά, with genitive [checked]
- **Confidence and review** · high · modified · for the editor

**Notes to keep**

- **B** · Retain the inclusive count, even though the preceding adjective says intermediate. The fourth has four notes and three steps, the fifth five and four, and the octave eight and seven. Boethius, De institutione musica I.16–17, ed. Friedlein (1867), pp. 201–204 [checked]
- **C** · Explain immediata and the etymological force of the three names. Keep sonitus as the sounding of the entire span here.

**Editor questions**

- Give the Greek-name glosses with the standard interval terms, in the text or the note, in a way that carries through the explicit etymology and inclusive counting.

### X30 · Xyl. 44.13–20

*Nam quia omnia intervalla complentur in octachordo, atque inde iterum geminantur, diapason dicitur, quod per octo nervos transit. Argumento sunt etiam nomina, quae sequentibus intervallis obtigerunt, ut sunt diapason cum diatessaron, diapason cum diapente: disdiapason, hoc est, octava cum quarta, octava cum quinta, et duae octavae.*

The octave embraces all the intervals within eight strings. Their pattern starts over from there. The names of the subsequent compound intervals provide further evidence for the naming.

- **Russian base** · A. Preserves the Greek–Latin glosses in readable Russian, though the repetition needs clearer wording.
- **English base** · B. Understands that the interval pattern repeats. Restore the meaningful distinctions between the Greek names and their Latin glosses.

**Divergence · atque inde iterum geminantur**

Do the interval sizes double, or does their pattern repeat?

- **B** · The intervals are repeated from the octave onwards.
  - B · RU «Поскольку все интервалы исчерпываются в октахорде, а затем повторяются, октава называется «через все», так как проходит через восемь струн. Об этом свидетельствуют и названия, доставшиеся следующим интервалам: октава с квартой, октава с квинтой и двойная октава, то есть октава вместе с квартой, октава вместе с квинтой и две октавы.» / EN “For all the intervals are comprised in the octachord and from there are repeated. The octave is called “through all” because it passes through eight strings. The names given to the following intervals are further evidence: the octave and a fourth, the octave and a fifth, and the double octave, that is, an octave with a fourth, an octave with a fifth, and two octaves.”
